========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doctor, I suppose what brings me in today is... well, it’s just this constant, heavy feeling I carry around with me now. Ever since my friend was killed, I’ve just been living with this awful mix of emotions that won't let up. It’s guilt, mostly, but also this deep fear, and anger. Oh, the anger is just always there, simmering underneath everything. I thought, and everyone told me—you know how people are, they always say, "Time heals all wounds," or "It’ll get easier with time"—but it hasn't. It hasn't eased up at all the way everyone seemed to think it would. I wake up with it, I go to sleep with it. It’s like a cloud over my head, this guilt about what happened, and the fear that something else terrible is going to happen, and I just... I can't seem to shake it, no matter how much time passes. It’s just exhausting, frankly, to feel this way every single day.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, we were... we had decided to take one of those walking tours downtown. We’d known each other for over forty years, ever since our undergraduate days, and we always tried to keep active and do things together. It was supposed to be just a lovely, normal afternoon. 

I'm sorry, it’s just so difficult to even articulate because of how it plays out in my mind now. You asked how it's affecting me specifically, and... it’s the reminders, doctor. They are absolutely everywhere, and I have no control over them. I could just be trying to run a simple errand, trying to go to the pharmacy or the grocer like a normal person, and suddenly there's a crosswalk signal. Just that ticking or chirping sound they make. Or I'll hear the growl of a truck, you know, that heavy, grinding engine noise when a delivery truck shifts gears down the street. Or, heaven forbid, I see a tourist holding a walking-tour map. 

Just seeing the way the paper folds, or hearing that engine... it suddenly puts the whole scene back in my head so sharply. It’s not just a passing thought, it’s... it’s like I’m standing right there on the pavement all over again. I can see my friend stepping off the curb before I can stop it. I see her foot going down onto the asphalt, and I try to reach out in my mind to pull her back, I try to force my brain to look away, but it just plays out exactly the same way. I can't stop the image. It just hijacks my mind completely, and I'll be standing there on the sidewalk, completely overwhelmed by this replay of her stepping off that curb, and I just can't seem to turn it off no matter how hard I try to tell myself that it's over and in the past.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, absolutely. I mean, I... I have to, don't I? If I don't, I just fall apart. So, for instance, that intersection—the one downtown where it happened—I absolutely will not go near it. I will literally drive miles out of my way, taking all these convoluted back roads and side streets, just to make sure I don't even come within a few blocks of it. My sister thinks I'm being ridiculous, she says, "It's just a street, you have to face it eventually," but she doesn't understand. She wasn't there. If I even think about turning down that avenue, my heart starts racing, and my hands get clammy on the steering wheel, and I just... I can't. 

And it's not just the intersection, either. I used to love those walking tours, as I mentioned, but I've stopped signing up for them entirely. Any of them. Even the ones in completely different neighborhoods or focused on different things, like the historical architecture tours I used to enjoy so much. I just can't do it. And the community center... I used to go to their safety meetings, you know, the neighborhood watch and pedestrian safety groups? I was quite involved. But I had to stop going to those, too. I just cannot bear it when the conversations circle back to what happened there. Because they always do, don't they? People mean well, they want to discuss crosswalk safety or traffic calming measures, but to me, it's not an abstract policy discussion. It's my friend. It's that awful day. So I just stay home. I stay home, and I avoid the tours, and I avoid the meetings, and I avoid that whole side of town, because it's the only way I can manage to get through the day without completely breaking down.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, constantly. It's... it's like a broken record in my head, doctor, just playing the same awful tune over and over until I think I might lose my mind. I just sit there in my armchair, sometimes for hours on end, and I keep going over and over the choices we made that afternoon. I keep telling myself that if I had just picked a different route—we could have walked down Elm Street instead, where the sidewalks are wider, or we could have just stayed inside the park boundaries where there are no cars at all. 

And that crossing... I knew it was a busy intersection. I knew it. I keep thinking that I should have pushed harder about it. I should have grabbed her arm, or said, "Let's wait for the next light," or "Let's walk down to the crosswalk with the better signal." If I had just done that, if I had just been a little more forceful or paid a bit more attention to where we were stepping, she might still be alive right now. We'd probably be having tea this afternoon like we always did on Tuesdays. 

But then... well, then the guilt sort of twists, doesn't it? It twists into this absolutely blinding anger. I get furious, just so intensely angry all over again at that driver. How could he not be paying attention? What on earth was so important that he couldn't look out for two older women crossing the street in broad daylight? And the town, too! I mean, the local council, the city planners—I get furious at them for letting it happen. They know that intersection is a nightmare. We've talked about it at those very safety meetings I used to go to! They knew the sightlines were terrible, they knew the delivery trucks moved too fast around that corner, and they just... they did absolutely nothing to fix it. They let it happen. And I just cycle through it all—blaming myself for the route, hating the driver, resenting the town council... it just never stops spinning in my head. I look at the world now and I just see incompetence and carelessness everywhere, and I can't seem to find any peace with people or myself anymore.
=====================================

Even the ones in completely different neighborhoods or focused on different things, like the historical architecture tours I used to enjoy so much. I just can't do it. And the community center... I used to go to their safety meetings, you know, the neighborhood watch and pedestrian safety groups? I was quite involved. But I had to stop going to those, too. I just cannot bear it when the conversations circle back to what happened there. Because they always do, don't they? People mean well, they want to discuss crosswalk safety or traffic calming measures, but to me, it's not an abstract policy discussion. It's my friend. It's that awful day. So I just stay home. I stay home, and I avoid the tours, and I avoid the meetings, and I avoid that whole side of town, because it's the only way I can manage to get through the day without completely breaking down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any ongoing negative thoughts about yourself, other people, or the world, especially since your friend’s death?\n\nPatient: Oh, constantly. It's... it's like a broken record in my head, doctor, just playing the same awful tune over and over until I think I might lose my mind. I just sit there in my armchair, sometimes for hours on end, and I keep going over and over the choices we made that afternoon. I keep telling myself that if I had just picked a different route—we could have walked down Elm Street instead, where the sidewalks are wider, or we could have just stayed inside the park boundaries where there are no cars at all. \n\nAnd that crossing... I knew it was a busy intersection. I knew it. I keep thinking that I should have pushed harder about it. I should have grabbed her arm, or said, \"Let's wait for the next light,\" or \"Let's walk down to the crosswalk with the better signal.\" If I had just done that, if I had just been a little more forceful or paid a bit more attention to where we were stepping, she might still be alive right now. We'd probably be having tea this afternoon like we always did on Tuesdays. \n\nBut then... well, then the guilt sort of twists, doesn't it? It twists into this absolutely blinding anger. I get furious, just so intensely angry all over again at that driver. How could he not be paying attention? What on earth was so important that he couldn't look out for two older women crossing the street in broad daylight? And the town, too! I mean, the local council, the city planners—I get furious at them for letting it happen. They know that intersection is a nightmare. We've talked about it at those very safety meetings I used to go to! They knew the sightlines were terrible, they knew the delivery trucks moved too fast around that corner, and they just... they did absolutely nothing to fix it. They let it happen. And I just cycle through it all—blaming myself for the route, hating the driver, resenting the town council... it just never stops spinning in my head. I look at the world now and I just see incompetence and carelessness everywhere, and I can't seem to find any peace with people or myself anymore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it hasn't stopped, doctor. Not for a single day since it happened. It’s been... oh, let me think, it was late summer when we went on that tour, so it’s been about two years and three months now. Two years and three months since she was killed on that walking tour. And you'd think, wouldn't you, that after more than two years, the edge would wear off just a little bit? That maybe I’d wake up one morning and the first thought in my head wouldn't be the sound of that truck or the sight of her stepping off the curb. But it doesn't. It just doesn't fade. 

Every single day for twenty-seven months, I’ve carried this heavy, dreadful feeling right here in my chest. And people say, "Oh, time heals all wounds," but they have absolutely no idea what they are talking about. How can time heal it when the guilt is just as fresh as it was that very afternoon? I still sit in my chair, just like I did the week after the funeral, going over the exact same "what ifs" and "if onlys." And the anger... well, the anger might actually be worse now than it was at the beginning. Because at the beginning, you're just in shock, aren't you? You're just trying to plan a funeral and figure out how to tell people. But now, two years and three months later, the shock is gone, and all I'm left with is this boiling rage at the driver, at the city council, at myself... and the fear, of course. The absolute terror of just walking down a normal street. It's been two years and three months of this, doctor, and I honestly don't know how much longer I can keep living like this. It's exhausting. It is just completely exhausting to carry this around every minute of every day since that afternoon.

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n5\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.11, F43.12\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I suppose what brings me in today is... well, it’s just this constant, heavy feeling I carry around with me now. Ever since my friend was killed, I’ve just been living with this awful mix of emotions that won't let up. It’s guilt, mostly, but also this deep fear, and anger. Oh, the anger is just always there, simmering underneath everything. I thought, and everyone told me—you know how people are, they always say, \"Time heals all wounds,\" or \"It’ll get easier with time\"—but it hasn't. It hasn't eased up at all the way everyone seemed to think it would. I wake up with it, I go to sleep with it. It’s like a cloud over my head, this guilt about what happened, and the fear that something else terrible is going to happen, and I just... I can't seem to shake it, no matter how much time passes. It’s just exhausting, frankly, to feel this way every single day.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about the event surrounding your friend’s death and how it has been affecting you specifically?\n\nPatient: Well, we were... we had decided to take one of those walking tours downtown. We’d known each other for over forty years, ever since our undergraduate days, and we always tried to keep active and do things together. It was supposed to be just a lovely, normal afternoon. \n\nI'm sorry, it’s just so difficult to even articulate because of how it plays out in my mind now. You asked how it's affecting me specifically, and... it’s the reminders, doctor. They are absolutely everywhere, and I have no control over them. I could just be trying to run a simple errand, trying to go to the pharmacy or the grocer like a normal person, and suddenly there's a crosswalk signal. Just that ticking or chirping sound they make. Or I'll hear the growl of a truck, you know, that heavy, grinding engine noise when a delivery truck shifts gears down the street. Or, heaven forbid, I see a tourist holding a walking-tour map. \n\nJust seeing the way the paper folds, or hearing that engine... it suddenly puts the whole scene back in my head so sharply. It’s not just a passing thought, it’s... it’s like I’m standing right there on the pavement all over again. I can see my friend stepping off the curb before I can stop it. I see her foot going down onto the asphalt, and I try to reach out in my mind to pull her back, I try to force my brain to look away, but it just plays out exactly the same way. I can't stop the image. It just hijacks my mind completely, and I'll be standing there on the sidewalk, completely overwhelmed by this replay of her stepping off that curb, and I just can't seem to turn it off no matter how hard I try to tell myself that it's over and in the past.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of what happened to your friend?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. I mean, I... I have to, don't I? If I don't, I just fall apart. So, for instance, that intersection—the one downtown where it happened—I absolutely will not go near it. I will literally drive miles out of my way, taking all these convoluted back roads and side streets, just to make sure I don't even come within a few blocks of it. My sister thinks I'm being ridiculous, she says, \"It's just a street, you have to face it eventually,\" but she doesn't understand. She wasn't there. If I even think about turning down that avenue, my heart starts racing, and my hands get clammy on the steering wheel, and I just... I can't. \n\nAnd it's not just the intersection, either. I used to love those walking tours, as I mentioned, but I've stopped signing up for them entirely. Any of them. Even the ones in completely different neighborhoods or focused on different things, like the historical architecture tours I used to enjoy so much. I just can't do it. And the community center... I used to go to their safety meetings, you know, the neighborhood watch and pedestrian safety groups? I was quite involved. But I had to stop going to those, too. I just cannot bear it when the conversations circle back to what happened there. Because they always do, don't they? People mean well, they want to discuss crosswalk safety or traffic calming measures, but to me, it's not an abstract policy discussion. It's my friend. It's that awful day. So I just stay home. I stay home, and I avoid the tours, and I avoid the meetings, and I avoid that whole side of town, because it's the only way I can manage to get through the day without completely breaking down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any ongoing negative thoughts about yourself, other people, or the world, especially since your friend’s death?\n\nPatient: Oh, constantly. It's... it's like a broken record in my head, doctor, just playing the same awful tune over and over until I think I might lose my mind. I just sit there in my armchair, sometimes for hours on end, and I keep going over and over the choices we made that afternoon. I keep telling myself that if I had just picked a different route—we could have walked down Elm Street instead, where the sidewalks are wider, or we could have just stayed inside the park boundaries where there are no cars at all. \n\nAnd that crossing... I knew it was a busy intersection. I knew it. I keep thinking that I should have pushed harder about it. I should have grabbed her arm, or said, \"Let's wait for the next light,\" or \"Let's walk down to the crosswalk with the better signal.\" If I had just done that, if I had just been a little more forceful or paid a bit more attention to where we were stepping, she might still be alive right now. We'd probably be having tea this afternoon like we always did on Tuesdays. \n\nBut then... well, then the guilt sort of twists, doesn't it? It twists into this absolutely blinding anger. I get furious, just so intensely angry all over again at that driver. How could he not be paying attention? What on earth was so important that he couldn't look out for two older women crossing the street in broad daylight? And the town, too! I mean, the local council, the city planners—I get furious at them for letting it happen. They know that intersection is a nightmare. We've talked about it at those very safety meetings I used to go to! They knew the sightlines were terrible, they knew the delivery trucks moved too fast around that corner, and they just... they did absolutely nothing to fix it. They let it happen. And I just cycle through it all—blaming myself for the route, hating the driver, resenting the town council... it just never stops spinning in my head. I look at the world now and I just see incompetence and carelessness everywhere, and I can't seem to find any peace with people or myself anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this constant guilt, fear, and anger—since your friend’s death?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't stopped, doctor. Not for a single day since it happened. It’s been... oh, let me think, it was late summer when we went on that tour, so it’s been about two years and three months now. Two years and three months since she was killed on that walking tour. And you'd think, wouldn't you, that after more than two years, the edge would wear off just a little bit? That maybe I’d wake up one morning and the first thought in my head wouldn't be the sound of that truck or the sight of her stepping off the curb. But it doesn't. It just doesn't fade. \n\nEvery single day for twenty-seven months, I’ve carried this heavy, dreadful feeling right here in my chest. I still sit in my chair, just like I did the week after the funeral, going over the exact same \"what ifs\" and \"if onlys.\" And the anger... well, the anger might actually be worse now than it was at the beginning. Because at the beginning, you're just in shock, aren't you? You're just trying to plan a funeral and figure out how to tell people. It is just completely exhausting to carry this around every minute of every day since that afternoon.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I just sit there in my armchair, sometimes for hours on end, and I keep going over and over the choices we made that afternoon. I keep telling myself that if I had just picked a different route—we could have walked down Elm Street instead, where the sidewalks are wider, or we could have just stayed inside the park boundaries where there are no cars at all. \n\nAnd that crossing... I knew it was a busy intersection. I knew it. I keep thinking that I should have pushed harder about it. I should have grabbed her arm, or said, \"Let's wait for the next light,\" or \"Let's walk down to the crosswalk with the better signal.\" If I had just done that, if I had just been a little more forceful or paid a bit more attention to where we were stepping, she might still be alive right now. We'd probably be having tea this afternoon like we always did on Tuesdays. \n\nBut then... well, then the guilt sort of twists, doesn't it? It twists into this absolutely blinding anger. I get furious, just so intensely angry all over again at that driver. How could he not be paying attention? What on earth was so important that he couldn't look out for two older women crossing the street in broad daylight? And the town, too! I mean, the local council, the city planners—I get furious at them for letting it happen. They know that intersection is a nightmare. We've talked about it at those very safety meetings I used to go to! They knew the sightlines were terrible, they knew the delivery trucks moved too fast around that corner, and they just... they did absolutely nothing to fix it. They let it happen. And I just cycle through it all—blaming myself for the route, hating the driver, resenting the town council... it just never stops spinning in my head. I look at the world now and I just see incompetence and carelessness everywhere, and I can't seem to find any peace with people or myself anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this constant guilt, fear, and anger—since your friend’s death?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't stopped, doctor. Not for a single day since it happened. It’s been... oh, let me think, it was late summer when we went on that tour, so it’s been about two years and three months now. Two years and three months since she was killed on that walking tour. And you'd think, wouldn't you, that after more than two years, the edge would wear off just a little bit? That maybe I’d wake up one morning and the first thought in my head wouldn't be the sound of that truck or the sight of her stepping off the curb. But it doesn't. It just doesn't fade. \n\nEvery single day for twenty-seven months, I’ve carried this heavy, dreadful feeling right here in my chest. And people say, \"Oh, time heals all wounds,\" but they have absolutely no idea what they are talking about. How can time heal it when the guilt is just as fresh as it was that very afternoon? I still sit in my chair, just like I did the week after the funeral, going over the exact same \"what ifs\" and \"if onlys.\" And the anger... well, the anger might actually be worse now than it was at the beginning. Because at the beginning, you're just in shock, aren't you? You're just trying to plan a funeral and figure out how to tell people. But now, two years and three months later, the shock is gone, and all I'm left with is this boiling rage at the driver, at the city council, at myself... and the fear, of course. The absolute terror of just walking down a normal street. It's been two years and three months of this, doctor, and I honestly don't know how much longer I can keep living like this. It's exhausting. It is just completely exhausting to carry this around every minute of every day since that afternoon.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you felt this way—this constant guilt, fear, and anger— every day since your friend’s death, without any significant breaks or relief?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes. Yes, I suppose that's exactly what I've been trying to say. Every single day. I mean, it's not like I don't try to find some relief, doctor, I really do. I try to distract myself. I'll sit down with a book—I used to be such an avid reader, mysteries mostly, Agatha Christie, that sort of thing—but I'll read the same paragraph over and over and realize I haven't absorbed a single word because my mind is right back at that intersection. Or I'll turn on the television, just for some background noise, hoping maybe a cooking show or a documentary will take my mind off it, but the relief just never comes. 

There's no break. It's not like I have a good week and then a bad week. It's just... a constant, heavy blanket over everything. Even when I'm sleeping, if you can call it sleeping, I wake up with my heart pounding and that same terrible feeling in the pit of my stomach. My sister—the one who thinks I'm being ridiculous about avoiding the street—she tells me I need to "take a holiday" or "find a new hobby" to give myself a break. As if a weekend at the coast is going to magically erase the fact that my dearest friend was killed right in front of me while we were just out for a walk! It's so frustrating trying to explain it to people who haven't lived it. They think you can just switch it off for a few hours and have a nice cup of tea and forget about it. But you can't. You just can't. It's always there, humming in the background, this awful mixture of feeling responsible and being terrified and just so incredibly angry. Every day. Every single day for two years and three months. There hasn't been a single day of peace since it happened.

My sister thinks I'm being ridiculous, she says, \"It's just a street, you have to face it eventually,\" but she doesn't understand. She wasn't there. If I even think about turning down that avenue, my heart starts racing, and my hands get clammy on the steering wheel, and I just... I can't. \n\nAnd it's not just the intersection, either. I used to love those walking tours, as I mentioned, but I've stopped signing up for them entirely. Any of them. Even the ones in completely different neighborhoods or focused on different things, like the historical architecture tours I used to enjoy so much. I just can't do it. And the community center... I used to go to their safety meetings, you know, the neighborhood watch and pedestrian safety groups? I was quite involved. But I had to stop going to those, too. I just cannot bear it when the conversations circle back to what happened there. Because they always do, don't they? People mean well, they want to discuss crosswalk safety or traffic calming measures, but to me, it's not an abstract policy discussion. It's my friend. It's that awful day. So I just stay home. I stay home, and I avoid the tours, and I avoid the meetings, and I avoid that whole side of town, because it's the only way I can manage to get through the day without completely breaking down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any ongoing negative thoughts about yourself, other people, or the world, especially since your friend’s death?\n\nPatient: Oh, constantly. It's... it's like a broken record in my head, doctor, just playing the same awful tune over and over until I think I might lose my mind. I just sit there in my armchair, sometimes for hours on end, and I keep going over and over the choices we made that afternoon. I keep telling myself that if I had just picked a different route—we could have walked down Elm Street instead, where the sidewalks are wider, or we could have just stayed inside the park boundaries where there are no cars at all. \n\nAnd that crossing... I knew it was a busy intersection. I knew it. I keep thinking that I should have pushed harder about it. I should have grabbed her arm, or said, \"Let's wait for the next light,\" or \"Let's walk down to the crosswalk with the better signal.\" If I had just done that, if I had just been a little more forceful or paid a bit more attention to where we were stepping, she might still be alive right now. We'd probably be having tea this afternoon like we always did on Tuesdays. \n\nBut then... well, then the guilt sort of twists, doesn't it? It twists into this absolutely blinding anger. I get furious, just so intensely angry all over again at that driver. How could he not be paying attention? What on earth was so important that he couldn't look out for two older women crossing the street in broad daylight? And the town, too! I mean, the local council, the city planners—I get furious at them for letting it happen. They know that intersection is a nightmare. We've talked about it at those very safety meetings I used to go to! They knew the sightlines were terrible, they knew the delivery trucks moved too fast around that corner, and they just... they did absolutely nothing to fix it. They let it happen. And I just cycle through it all—blaming myself for the route, hating the driver, resenting the town council... it just never stops spinning in my head. I look at the world now and I just see incompetence and carelessness everywhere, and I can't seem to find any peace with people or myself anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this constant guilt, fear, and anger—since your friend’s death?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't stopped, doctor. Not for a single day since it happened. It’s been... oh, let me think, it was late summer when we went on that tour, so it’s been about two years and three months now. Two years and three months since she was killed on that walking tour. And you'd think, wouldn't you, that after more than two years, the edge would wear off just a little bit? That maybe I’d wake up one morning and the first thought in my head wouldn't be the sound of that truck or the sight of her stepping off the curb. But it doesn't. It just doesn't fade. \n\nEvery single day for twenty-seven months, I’ve carried this heavy, dreadful feeling right here in my chest. And people say, \"Oh, time heals all wounds,\" but they have absolutely no idea what they are talking about. How can time heal it when the guilt is just as fresh as it was that very afternoon? I still sit in my chair, just like I did the week after the funeral, going over the exact same \"what ifs\" and \"if onlys.\" And the anger... well, the anger might actually be worse now than it was at the beginning. Because at the beginning, you're just in shock, aren't you? You're just trying to plan a funeral and figure out how to tell people. But now, two years and three months later, the shock is gone, and all I'm left with is this boiling rage at the driver, at the city council, at myself... and the fear, of course. The absolute terror of just walking down a normal street. It's been two years and three months of this, doctor, and I honestly don't know how much longer I can keep living like this. It's exhausting. It is just completely exhausting to carry this around every minute of every day since that afternoon.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way—this constant guilt, fear, and anger— every day since your friend’s death, without any significant breaks or relief?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, I suppose that's exactly what I've been trying to say. Every single day. I mean, it's not like I don't try to find some relief, doctor, I really do. I try to distract myself. I'll sit down with a book—I used to be such an avid reader, mysteries mostly, Agatha Christie, that sort of thing—but I'll read the same paragraph over and over and realize I haven't absorbed a single word because my mind is right back at that intersection. Or I'll turn on the television, just for some background noise, hoping maybe a cooking show or a documentary will take my mind off it, but the relief just never comes. \n\nThere's no break. It's not like I have a good week and then a bad week. It's just... a constant, heavy blanket over everything. Even when I'm sleeping, if you can call it sleeping, I wake up with my heart pounding and that same terrible feeling in the pit of my stomach. My sister—the one who thinks I'm being ridiculous about avoiding the street—she tells me I need to \"take a holiday\" or \"find a new hobby\" to give myself a break. As if a weekend at the coast is going to magically erase the fact that my dearest friend was killed right in front of me while we were just out for a walk! It's so frustrating trying to explain it to people who haven't lived it. They think you can just switch it off for a few hours and have a nice cup of tea and forget about it. But you can't. You just can't. It's always there, humming in the background, this awful mixture of feeling responsible and being terrified and just so incredibly angry. Every day. Every single day for two years and three months. There hasn't been a single day of peace since it happened.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes... yes, that's exactly it. It's like all the color has just completely drained out of everything. I used to have such a full schedule, doctor, you wouldn't even believe it. My calendar was always marked up with different colored pens for all my different groups and projects. 

I used to spend hours in the late winter just planning my garden beds—mapping out exactly where the dahlias would go, making sure the hostas had enough shade, ordering seeds from those specialty catalogs... it gave me such a thrill to see it all come together. And then there were my quilting ladies. We'd get together every Thursday morning, bringing our scraps and our sewing machines, and we'd just piece together these beautiful tops for hours. We even organized these lovely theater outings once a month, getting a group rate for the matinees downtown. We saw some truly wonderful productions. 

And the book club! I mentioned earlier that I used to read a lot of mysteries, well, we had this little group and I used to just love it. I would argue happily—just so passionately—with the other women about the plot holes or who the killer was in whatever book we were reading that month. We'd sit around and just debate the clues, having such a marvelous time. 

But now... now I just don't care. I look at those same things on the calendar—well, the old calendars, because I hardly bother writing things down anymore—and I feel almost nothing. It's just completely blank inside. The ladies still call, bless them, they ask if I'm coming back to the quilt group, or if I want a ticket to the new play, and I just make an excuse. Because I look at those activities, those things that used to fill my days with so much joy, and it's like... it's like they belong to someone else entirely. Like they belong to someone I used to be, before that afternoon. The thought of sitting in a theater or arguing over a fictional story just feels so utterly pointless now. I just can't muster a single ounce of pleasure for any of it.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I suppose what brings me in today is... well, it’s just this constant, heavy feeling I carry around with me now. Ever since my friend was killed, I’ve just been living with this awful mix of emotions that won't let up. It’s guilt, mostly, but also this deep fear, and anger. Oh, the anger is just always there, simmering underneath everything. I thought, and everyone told me—you know how people are, they always say, \"Time heals all wounds,\" or \"It’ll get easier with time\"—but it hasn't. It hasn't eased up at all the way everyone seemed to think it would. I wake up with it, I go to sleep with it. It’s like a cloud over my head, this guilt about what happened, and the fear that something else terrible is going to happen, and I just... I can't seem to shake it, no matter how much time passes. It’s just exhausting, frankly, to feel this way every single day.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about the event surrounding your friend’s death and how it has been affecting you specifically?\n\nPatient: Well, we were... we had decided to take one of those walking tours downtown. We’d known each other for over forty years, ever since our undergraduate days, and we always tried to keep active and do things together. It was supposed to be just a lovely, normal afternoon. \n\nI'm sorry, it’s just so difficult to even articulate because of how it plays out in my mind now. You asked how it's affecting me specifically, and... it’s the reminders, doctor. They are absolutely everywhere, and I have no control over them. I could just be trying to run a simple errand, trying to go to the pharmacy or the grocer like a normal person, and suddenly there's a crosswalk signal. Just that ticking or chirping sound they make. Or I'll hear the growl of a truck, you know, that heavy, grinding engine noise when a delivery truck shifts gears down the street. Or, heaven forbid, I see a tourist holding a walking-tour map. \n\nJust seeing the way the paper folds, or hearing that engine... it suddenly puts the whole scene back in my head so sharply. It’s not just a passing thought, it’s... it’s like I’m standing right there on the pavement all over again. I can see my friend stepping off the curb before I can stop it. I see her foot going down onto the asphalt, and I try to reach out in my mind to pull her back, I try to force my brain to look away, but it just plays out exactly the same way. I can't stop the image. It just hijacks my mind completely, and I'll be standing there on the sidewalk, completely overwhelmed by this replay of her stepping off that curb, and I just can't seem to turn it off no matter how hard I try to tell myself that it's over and in the past.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of what happened to your friend?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. I mean, I... I have to, don't I? If I don't, I just fall apart. So, for instance, that intersection—the one downtown where it happened—I absolutely will not go near it. I will literally drive miles out of my way, taking all these convoluted back roads and side streets, just to make sure I don't even come within a few blocks of it. My sister thinks I'm being ridiculous, she says, \"It's just a street, you have to face it eventually,\" but she doesn't understand. She wasn't there. If I even think about turning down that avenue, my heart starts racing, and my hands get clammy on the steering wheel, and I just... I can't. \n\nAnd it's not just the intersection, either. I used to love those walking tours, as I mentioned, but I've stopped signing up for them entirely. Any of them. Even the ones in completely different neighborhoods or focused on different things, like the historical architecture tours I used to enjoy so much. I just can't do it. And the community center... I used to go to their safety meetings, you know, the neighborhood watch and pedestrian safety groups? I was quite involved. But I had to stop going to those, too. I just cannot bear it when the conversations circle back to what happened there. Because they always do, don't they? People mean well, they want to discuss crosswalk safety or traffic calming measures, but to me, it's not an abstract policy discussion. It's my friend. It's that awful day. So I just stay home. I stay home, and I avoid the tours, and I avoid the meetings, and I avoid that whole side of town, because it's the only way I can manage to get through the day without completely breaking down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any ongoing negative thoughts about yourself, other people, or the world, especially since your friend’s death?\n\nPatient: Oh, constantly. It's... it's like a broken record in my head, doctor, just playing the same awful tune over and over until I think I might lose my mind. I just sit there in my armchair, sometimes for hours on end, and I keep going over and over the choices we made that afternoon. I keep telling myself that if I had just picked a different route—we could have walked down Elm Street instead, where the sidewalks are wider, or we could have just stayed inside the park boundaries where there are no cars at all. \n\nAnd that crossing... I knew it was a busy intersection. I knew it. I keep thinking that I should have pushed harder about it. I should have grabbed her arm, or said, \"Let's wait for the next light,\" or \"Let's walk down to the crosswalk with the better signal.\" If I had just done that, if I had just been a little more forceful or paid a bit more attention to where we were stepping, she might still be alive right now. We'd probably be having tea this afternoon like we always did on Tuesdays. \n\nBut then... well, then the guilt sort of twists, doesn't it? It twists into this absolutely blinding anger. I get furious, just so intensely angry all over again at that driver. How could he not be paying attention? What on earth was so important that he couldn't look out for two older women crossing the street in broad daylight? And the town, too! I mean, the local council, the city planners—I get furious at them for letting it happen. They know that intersection is a nightmare. We've talked about it at those very safety meetings I used to go to! They knew the sightlines were terrible, they knew the delivery trucks moved too fast around that corner, and they just... they did absolutely nothing to fix it. They let it happen. And I just cycle through it all—blaming myself for the route, hating the driver, resenting the town council... it just never stops spinning in my head. I look at the world now and I just see incompetence and carelessness everywhere, and I can't seem to find any peace with people or myself anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this constant guilt, fear, and anger—since your friend’s death?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't stopped, doctor. Not for a single day since it happened. It’s been... oh, let me think, it was late summer when we went on that tour, so it’s been about two years and three months now. Two years and three months since she was killed on that walking tour. And you'd think, wouldn't you, that after more than two years, the edge would wear off just a little bit? That maybe I’d wake up one morning and the first thought in my head wouldn't be the sound of that truck or the sight of her stepping off the curb. But it doesn't. It just doesn't fade. \n\nEvery single day for twenty-seven months, I’ve carried this heavy, dreadful feeling right here in my chest. And people say, \"Oh, time heals all wounds,\" but they have absolutely no idea what they are talking about. How can time heal it when the guilt is just as fresh as it was that very afternoon? I still sit in my chair, just like I did the week after the funeral, going over the exact same \"what ifs\" and \"if onlys.\" And the anger... well, the anger might actually be worse now than it was at the beginning. Because at the beginning, you're just in shock, aren't you? You're just trying to plan a funeral and figure out how to tell people. But now, two years and three months later, the shock is gone, and all I'm left with is this boiling rage at the driver, at the city council, at myself... and the fear, of course. The absolute terror of just walking down a normal street. It's been two years and three months of this, doctor, and I honestly don't know how much longer I can keep living like this. It's exhausting. It is just completely exhausting to carry this around every minute of every day since that afternoon.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way—this constant guilt, fear, and anger— every day since your friend’s death, without any significant breaks or relief?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, I suppose that's exactly what I've been trying to say. Every single day. I mean, it's not like I don't try to find some relief, doctor, I really do. I try to distract myself. I'll sit down with a book—I used to be such an avid reader, mysteries mostly, Agatha Christie, that sort of thing—but I'll read the same paragraph over and over and realize I haven't absorbed a single word because my mind is right back at that intersection. Or I'll turn on the television, just for some background noise, hoping maybe a cooking show or a documentary will take my mind off it, but the relief just never comes. \n\nThere's no break. It's not like I have a good week and then a bad week. It's just... a constant, heavy blanket over everything. Even when I'm sleeping, if you can call it sleeping, I wake up with my heart pounding and that same terrible feeling in the pit of my stomach. My sister—the one who thinks I'm being ridiculous about avoiding the street—she tells me I need to \"take a holiday\" or \"find a new hobby\" to give myself a break. As if a weekend at the coast is going to magically erase the fact that my dearest friend was killed right in front of me while we were just out for a walk! It's so frustrating trying to explain it to people who haven't lived it. They think you can just switch it off for a few hours and have a nice cup of tea and forget about it. But you can't. You just can't. It's always there, humming in the background, this awful mixture of feeling responsible and being terrified and just so incredibly angry. Every day. Every single day for two years and three months. There hasn't been a single day of peace since it happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy, or have you found that you can’t feel pleasure in things that used to make you happy?\n\nPatient: Yes... yes, that's exactly it. It's like all the color has just completely drained out of everything. I used to have such a full schedule, doctor, you wouldn't even believe it. My calendar was always marked up with different colored pens for all my different groups and projects. \n\nI used to spend hours in the late winter just planning my garden beds—mapping out exactly where the dahlias would go, making sure the hostas had enough shade, ordering seeds from those specialty catalogs... it gave me such a thrill to see it all come together. And then there were my quilting ladies. We'd get together every Thursday morning, bringing our scraps and our sewing machines, and we'd just piece together these beautiful tops for hours. We even organized these lovely theater outings once a month, getting a group rate for the matinees downtown. We saw some truly wonderful productions. \n\nAnd the book club! I mentioned earlier that I used to read a lot of mysteries, well, we had this little group and I used to just love it. I would argue happily—just so passionately—with the other women about the plot holes or who the killer was in whatever book we were reading that month. We'd sit around and just debate the clues, having such a marvelous time. \n\nBut now... now I just don't care. I look at those same things on the calendar—well, the old calendars, because I hardly bother writing things down anymore—and I feel almost nothing. It's just completely blank inside. The ladies still call, bless them, they ask if I'm coming back to the quilt group, or if I want a ticket to the new play, and I just make an excuse. Because I look at those activities, those things that used to fill my days with so much joy, and it's like... it's like they belong to someone else entirely. Like they belong to someone I used to be, before that afternoon. The thought of sitting in a theater or arguing over a fictional story just feels so utterly pointless now. I just can't muster a single ounce of pleasure for any of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

  }

3 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since my friend was killed on that walking tour about two years and three months ago.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 69 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S074\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this constant guilt, fear, and anger—since your friend’s death?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't stopped, doctor. Not for a single day since it happened. It’s been... oh, let me think, it was late summer when we went on that tour, so it’s been about two years and three months now. Two years and three months since she was killed on that walking tour. And you'd think, wouldn't you, that after more than two years, the edge would wear off just a little bit? That maybe I’d wake up one morning and the first thought in my head wouldn't be the sound of that truck or the sight of her stepping off the curb. But it doesn't. It just doesn't fade. \n\nEvery single day for twenty-seven months, I’ve carried this heavy, dreadful feeling right here in my chest. And people say, \"Oh, time heals all wounds,\" but they have absolutely no idea what they are talking about. How can time heal it when the guilt is just as fresh as it was that very afternoon? I still sit in my chair, just like I did the week after the funeral, going over the exact same \"what ifs\" and \"if onlys.\" And the anger... well, the anger might actually be worse now than it was at the beginning. Because at the beginning, you're just in shock, aren't you? You're just trying to plan a funeral and figure out how to tell people. But now, two years and three months later, the shock is gone, and all I'm left with is this boiling rage at the driver, at the city council, at myself... and the fear, of course. The absolute terror of just walking down a normal street. It's been two years and three months of this, doctor, and I honestly don't know how much longer I can keep living like this. It's exhausting. It is just completely exhausting to carry this around every minute of every day since that afternoon.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way—this constant guilt, fear, and anger— every day since your friend’s death, without any significant breaks or relief?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, I suppose that's exactly what I've been trying to say. Every single day. I mean, it's not like I don't try to find some relief, doctor, I really do. I try to distract myself. I'll sit down with a book—I used to be such an avid reader, mysteries mostly, Agatha Christie, that sort of thing—but I'll read the same paragraph over and over and realize I haven't absorbed a single word because my mind is right back at that intersection. Or I'll turn on the television, just for some background noise, hoping maybe a cooking show or a documentary will take my mind off it, but the relief just never comes. \n\nThere's no break. It's not like I have a good week and then a bad week. It's just... a constant, heavy blanket over everything. Even when I'm sleeping, if you can call it sleeping, I wake up with my heart pounding and that same terrible feeling in the pit of my stomach. My sister—the one who thinks I'm being ridiculous about avoiding the street—she tells me I need to \"take a holiday\" or \"find a new hobby\" to give myself a break. As if a weekend at the coast is going to magically erase the fact that my dearest friend was killed right in front of me while we were just out for a walk! It's so frustrating trying to explain it to people who haven't lived it. They think you can just switch it off for a few hours and have a nice cup of tea and forget about it. But you can't. You just can't. It's always there, humming in the background, this awful mixture of feeling responsible and being terrified and just so incredibly angry. Every day. Every single day for two years and three months. There hasn't been a single day of peace since it happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy, or have you found that you can’t feel pleasure in things that used to make you happy?\n\nPatient: Yes... yes, that's exactly it. It's like all the color has just completely drained out of everything. I used to have such a full schedule, doctor, you wouldn't even believe it. My calendar was always marked up with different colored pens for all my different groups and projects. \n\nI used to spend hours in the late winter just planning my garden beds—mapping out exactly where the dahlias would go, making sure the hostas had enough shade, ordering seeds from those specialty catalogs... it gave me such a thrill to see it all come together. And then there were my quilting ladies. We'd get together every Thursday morning, bringing our scraps and our sewing machines, and we'd just piece together these beautiful tops for hours. We even organized these lovely theater outings once a month, getting a group rate for the matinees downtown. We saw some truly wonderful productions. \n\nAnd the book club! I mentioned earlier that I used to read a lot of mysteries, well, we had this little group and I used to just love it. I would argue happily—just so passionately—with the other women about the plot holes or who the killer was in whatever book we were reading that month. We'd sit around and just debate the clues, having such a marvelous time. \n\nBut now... now I just don't care. I look at those same things on the calendar—well, the old calendars, because I hardly bother writing things down anymore—and I feel almost nothing. It's just completely blank inside. The ladies still call, bless them, they ask if I'm coming back to the quilt group, or if I want a ticket to the new play, and I just make an excuse. Because I look at those activities, those things that used to fill my days with so much joy, and it's like... it's like they belong to someone else entirely. Like they belong to someone I used to be, before that afternoon. The thought of sitting in a theater or arguing over a fictional story just feels so utterly pointless now. I just can't muster a single ounce of pleasure for any of it.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you describe your mood most days—are you mostly sad, angry, or do your emotions shift between different states?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it is not really a shift, doctor, not like a pendulum swinging back and forth between different things. It is more like a constant, awful mixture of all of them at once. What really brings me in here to see you, what I have been trying to make everyone understand, is that I have been living with this constant mix of guilt, fear, and anger since my friend was killed. It is all of them, all the time, just sitting right here heavily in my chest. 

I wake up in the morning, and the fear is right there, the fear of stepping outside, of the traffic, of just existing in a world where something so terrible can happen in the blink of an eye. And then the guilt just washes over me, because I was there, wasn't I? I was right beside her, and I lived, and she didn't. I have spent hours, oh, countless hours, just sitting in my armchair in the living room, the one by the window with the floral upholstery, looking out at the street and feeling so incredibly guilty that I am the one still breathing. And the anger is always boiling underneath it all, anger at the driver, anger at the whole situation. 

And the thing is, doctor, it just has not eased. It has not eased at all, not the way everyone seemed to think it would. People have these timelines in their heads, you see. They think that after a year, I should be moving on, or that after two years, surely I am finding my peace by now. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she actually had the nerve to tell me a few months ago that I needed to let go of the past because holding onto it was only hurting me. Can you imagine? As if I am holding onto this on purpose! As if I want to wake up every single day feeling this dreadful combination of being terrified and furious and so very guilty. They just do not understand that it does not just wash away. It is a constant state of being. I am just stuck in it, every single day since that awful afternoon.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.11, F43.12\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I suppose what brings me in today is... well, it’s just this constant, heavy feeling I carry around with me now. Ever since my friend was killed, I’ve just been living with this awful mix of emotions that won't let up. It’s guilt, mostly, but also this deep fear, and anger. Oh, the anger is just always there, simmering underneath everything. I thought, and everyone told me—you know how people are, they always say, \"Time heals all wounds,\" or \"It’ll get easier with time\"—but it hasn't. It hasn't eased up at all the way everyone seemed to think it would. I wake up with it, I go to sleep with it. It’s like a cloud over my head, this guilt about what happened, and the fear that something else terrible is going to happen, and I just... I can't seem to shake it, no matter how much time passes. It’s just exhausting, frankly, to feel this way every single day.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about the event surrounding your friend’s death and how it has been affecting you specifically?\n\nPatient: Well, we were... we had decided to take one of those walking tours downtown. We’d known each other for over forty years, ever since our undergraduate days, and we always tried to keep active and do things together. It was supposed to be just a lovely, normal afternoon. \n\nI'm sorry, it’s just so difficult to even articulate because of how it plays out in my mind now. You asked how it's affecting me specifically, and... it’s the reminders, doctor. They are absolutely everywhere, and I have no control over them. I could just be trying to run a simple errand, trying to go to the pharmacy or the grocer like a normal person, and suddenly there's a crosswalk signal. Just that ticking or chirping sound they make. Or I'll hear the growl of a truck, you know, that heavy, grinding engine noise when a delivery truck shifts gears down the street. Or, heaven forbid, I see a tourist holding a walking-tour map. \n\nJust seeing the way the paper folds, or hearing that engine... it suddenly puts the whole scene back in my head so sharply. It’s not just a passing thought, it’s... it’s like I’m standing right there on the pavement all over again. I can see my friend stepping off the curb before I can stop it. I see her foot going down onto the asphalt, and I try to reach out in my mind to pull her back, I try to force my brain to look away, but it just plays out exactly the same way. I can't stop the image. It just hijacks my mind completely, and I'll be standing there on the sidewalk, completely overwhelmed by this replay of her stepping off that curb, and I just can't seem to turn it off no matter how hard I try to tell myself that it's over and in the past.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of what happened to your friend?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. I mean, I... I have to, don't I? If I don't, I just fall apart. So, for instance, that intersection—the one downtown where it happened—I absolutely will not go near it. I will literally drive miles out of my way, taking all these convoluted back roads and side streets, just to make sure I don't even come within a few blocks of it. My sister thinks I'm being ridiculous, she says, \"It's just a street, you have to face it eventually,\" but she doesn't understand. She wasn't there. If I even think about turning down that avenue, my heart starts racing, and my hands get clammy on the steering wheel, and I just... I can't. \n\nAnd it's not just the intersection, either. I used to love those walking tours, as I mentioned, but I've stopped signing up for them entirely. Any of them. Even the ones in completely different neighborhoods or focused on different things, like the historical architecture tours I used to enjoy so much. I just can't do it. And the community center... I used to go to their safety meetings, you know, the neighborhood watch and pedestrian safety groups? I was quite involved. But I had to stop going to those, too. I just cannot bear it when the conversations circle back to what happened there. Because they always do, don't they? People mean well, they want to discuss crosswalk safety or traffic calming measures, but to me, it's not an abstract policy discussion. It's my friend. It's that awful day. So I just stay home. I stay home, and I avoid the tours, and I avoid the meetings, and I avoid that whole side of town, because it's the only way I can manage to get through the day without completely breaking down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any ongoing negative thoughts about yourself, other people, or the world, especially since your friend’s death?\n\nPatient: Oh, constantly. It's... it's like a broken record in my head, doctor, just playing the same awful tune over and over until I think I might lose my mind. I just sit there in my armchair, sometimes for hours on end, and I keep going over and over the choices we made that afternoon. I keep telling myself that if I had just picked a different route—we could have walked down Elm Street instead, where the sidewalks are wider, or we could have just stayed inside the park boundaries where there are no cars at all. \n\nAnd that crossing... I knew it was a busy intersection. I knew it. I keep thinking that I should have pushed harder about it. I should have grabbed her arm, or said, \"Let's wait for the next light,\" or \"Let's walk down to the crosswalk with the better signal.\" If I had just done that, if I had just been a little more forceful or paid a bit more attention to where we were stepping, she might still be alive right now. We'd probably be having tea this afternoon like we always did on Tuesdays. \n\nBut then... well, then the guilt sort of twists, doesn't it? It twists into this absolutely blinding anger. I get furious, just so intensely angry all over again at that driver. How could he not be paying attention? What on earth was so important that he couldn't look out for two older women crossing the street in broad daylight? And the town, too! I mean, the local council, the city planners—I get furious at them for letting it happen. They know that intersection is a nightmare. We've talked about it at those very safety meetings I used to go to! They knew the sightlines were terrible, they knew the delivery trucks moved too fast around that corner, and they just... they did absolutely nothing to fix it. They let it happen. And I just cycle through it all—blaming myself for the route, hating the driver, resenting the town council... it just never stops spinning in my head. I look at the world now and I just see incompetence and carelessness everywhere, and I can't seem to find any peace with people or myself anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this constant guilt, fear, and anger—since your friend’s death?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't stopped, doctor. Not for a single day since it happened. It’s been... oh, let me think, it was late summer when we went on that tour, so it’s been about two years and three months now. Two years and three months since she was killed on that walking tour. And you'd think, wouldn't you, that after more than two years, the edge would wear off just a little bit? That maybe I’d wake up one morning and the first thought in my head wouldn't be the sound of that truck or the sight of her stepping off the curb. But it doesn't. It just doesn't fade. \n\nEvery single day for twenty-seven months, I’ve carried this heavy, dreadful feeling right here in my chest. And people say, \"Oh, time heals all wounds,\" but they have absolutely no idea what they are talking about. How can time heal it when the guilt is just as fresh as it was that very afternoon? I still sit in my chair, just like I did the week after the funeral, going over the exact same \"what ifs\" and \"if onlys.\" And the anger... well, the anger might actually be worse now than it was at the beginning. Because at the beginning, you're just in shock, aren't you? You're just trying to plan a funeral and figure out how to tell people. But now, two years and three months later, the shock is gone, and all I'm left with is this boiling rage at the driver, at the city council, at myself... and the fear, of course. The absolute terror of just walking down a normal street. It's been two years and three months of this, doctor, and I honestly don't know how much longer I can keep living like this. It's exhausting. It is just completely exhausting to carry this around every minute of every day since that afternoon.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way—this constant guilt, fear, and anger— every day since your friend’s death, without any significant breaks or relief?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. I'll sit down with a book—I used to be such an avid reader, mysteries mostly, Agatha Christie, that sort of thing—but I'll read the same paragraph over and over and realize I haven't absorbed a single word because my mind is right back at that intersection. Or I'll turn on the television, just for some background noise, hoping maybe a cooking show or a documentary will take my mind off it, but the relief just never comes. \n\nThere's no break. It's not like I have a good week and then a bad week. It's just... a constant, heavy blanket over everything. Even when I'm sleeping, if you can call it sleeping, I wake up with my heart pounding and that same terrible feeling in the pit of my stomach. My sister—the one who thinks I'm being ridiculous about avoiding the street—she tells me I need to \"take a holiday\" or \"find a new hobby\" to give myself a break. As if a weekend at the coast is going to magically erase the fact that my dearest friend was killed right in front of me while we were just out for a walk! It's so frustrating trying to explain it to people who haven't lived it. They think you can just switch it off for a few hours and have a nice cup of tea and forget about it. But you can't. You just can't. It's always there, humming in the background, this awful mixture of feeling responsible and being terrified and just so incredibly angry. Every day. Every single day for two years and three months. There hasn't been a single day of peace since it happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy, or have you found that you can’t feel pleasure in things that used to make you happy?\n\nPatient: Yes... yes, that's exactly it. We even organized these lovely theater outings once a month, getting a group rate for the matinees downtown. We saw some truly wonderful productions. \n\nAnd the book club! We'd sit around and just debate the clues, having such a marvelous time. \n\nBut now... now I just don't care. I look at those same things on the calendar—well, the old calendars, because I hardly bother writing things down anymore—and I feel almost nothing. It's just completely blank inside. The ladies still call, bless them, they ask if I'm coming back to the quilt group, or if I want a ticket to the new play, and I just make an excuse. Because I look at those activities, those things that used to fill my days with so much joy, and it's like... it's like they belong to someone else entirely. Like they belong to someone I used to be, before that afternoon. The thought of sitting in a theater or arguing over a fictional story just feels so utterly pointless now. I just can't muster a single ounce of pleasure for any of it.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe your mood most days—are you mostly sad, angry, or do your emotions shift between different states?\n\nPatient: Well, it is not really a shift, doctor, not like a pendulum swinging back and forth between different things. It is more like a constant, awful mixture of all of them at once. What really brings me in here to see you, what I have been trying to make everyone understand, is that I have been living with this constant mix of guilt, fear, and anger since my friend was killed. And I just cycle through it all—blaming myself for the route, hating the driver, resenting the town council... it just never stops spinning in my head. I look at the world now and I just see incompetence and carelessness everywhere, and I can't seem to find any peace with people or myself anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this constant guilt, fear, and anger—since your friend’s death?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't stopped, doctor. Not for a single day since it happened. It’s been... oh, let me think, it was late summer when we went on that tour, so it’s been about two years and three months now. Two years and three months since she was killed on that walking tour. And you'd think, wouldn't you, that after more than two years, the edge would wear off just a little bit? That maybe I’d wake up one morning and the first thought in my head wouldn't be the sound of that truck or the sight of her stepping off the curb. But it doesn't. It just doesn't fade. \n\nEvery single day for twenty-seven months, I’ve carried this heavy, dreadful feeling right here in my chest. The absolute terror of just walking down a normal street. It's been two years and three months of this, doctor, and I honestly don't know how much longer I can keep living like this. It's exhausting.
