It's not like I have a good week and then a bad week. It's just... a constant, heavy blanket over everything. Even when I'm sleeping, if you can call it sleeping, I wake up with my heart pounding and that same terrible feeling in the pit of my stomach. My sister—the one who thinks I'm being ridiculous about avoiding the street—she tells me I need to \"take a holiday\" or \"find a new hobby\" to give myself a break. As if a weekend at the coast is going to magically erase the fact that my dearest friend was killed right in front of me while we were just out for a walk! It's so frustrating trying to explain it to people who haven't lived it. They think you can just switch it off for a few hours and have a nice cup of tea and forget about it. But you can't. You just can't. It's always there, humming in the background, this awful mixture of feeling responsible and being terrified and just so incredibly angry. Every day. Every single day for two years and three months. There hasn't been a single day of peace since it happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy, or have you found that you can’t feel pleasure in things that used to make you happy?\n\nPatient: Yes... yes, that's exactly it. It's like all the color has just completely drained out of everything. I used to have such a full schedule, doctor, you wouldn't even believe it. My calendar was always marked up with different colored pens for all my different groups and projects. \n\nI used to spend hours in the late winter just planning my garden beds—mapping out exactly where the dahlias would go, making sure the hostas had enough shade, ordering seeds from those specialty catalogs... it gave me such a thrill to see it all come together. And then there were my quilting ladies. We'd get together every Thursday morning, bringing our scraps and our sewing machines, and we'd just piece together these beautiful tops for hours. We even organized these lovely theater outings once a month, getting a group rate for the matinees downtown. We saw some truly wonderful productions. \n\nAnd the book club! I mentioned earlier that I used to read a lot of mysteries, well, we had this little group and I used to just love it. I would argue happily—just so passionately—with the other women about the plot holes or who the killer was in whatever book we were reading that month. We'd sit around and just debate the clues, having such a marvelous time. \n\nBut now... now I just don't care. I look at those same things on the calendar—well, the old calendars, because I hardly bother writing things down anymore—and I feel almost nothing. It's just completely blank inside. The ladies still call, bless them, they ask if I'm coming back to the quilt group, or if I want a ticket to the new play, and I just make an excuse. Because I look at those activities, those things that used to fill my days with so much joy, and it's like... it's like they belong to someone else entirely. Like they belong to someone I used to be, before that afternoon. The thought of sitting in a theater or arguing over a fictional story just feels so utterly pointless now. I just can't muster a single ounce of pleasure for any of it.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe your mood most days—are you mostly sad, angry, or do your emotions shift between different states?\n\nPatient: Well, it is not really a shift, doctor, not like a pendulum swinging back and forth between different things. It is more like a constant, awful mixture of all of them at once. What really brings me in here to see you, what I have been trying to make everyone understand, is that I have been living with this constant mix of guilt, fear, and anger since my friend was killed. It is all of them, all the time, just sitting right here heavily in my chest. \n\nI wake up in the morning, and the fear is right there, the fear of stepping outside, of the traffic, of just existing in a world where something so terrible can happen in the blink of an eye. And then the guilt just washes over me, because I was there, wasn't I? I was right beside her, and I lived, and she didn't. I have spent hours, oh, countless hours, just sitting in my armchair in the living room, the one by the window with the floral upholstery, looking out at the street and feeling so incredibly guilty that I am the one still breathing. And the anger is always boiling underneath it all, anger at the driver, anger at the whole situation. \n\nAnd the thing is, doctor, it just has not eased. It has not eased at all, not the way everyone seemed to think it would. People have these timelines in their heads, you see. They think that after a year, I should be moving on, or that after two years, surely I am finding my peace by now. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she actually had the nerve to tell me a few months ago that I needed to let go of the past because holding onto it was only hurting me. Can you imagine? As if I am holding onto this on purpose! As if I want to wake up every single day feeling this dreadful combination of being terrified and furious and so very guilty. They just do not understand that it does not just wash away. It is a constant state of being. I am just stuck in it, every single day since that awful afternoon.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel down, depressed, or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 69 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, not... not exactly depressed or hopeless, doctor. I mean, I don't sit around crying all day or feeling like life is completely over, like there's no future at all. It's not that deep, dark sadness that you hear people talk about when they say they're depressed. It's just... well, like I was saying, it's the fear and the guilt and the anger that are so exhausting. 

I don't wake up feeling down in the dumps, exactly. I wake up feeling on edge. My sister, she called me just the other day—Tuesday, I think it was, right before lunch—and she asked me if I was feeling depressed because I hadn't returned her call from the weekend. And I had to tell her, "Margaret, I'm not depressed, I'm just tired." I'm so very tired of carrying this awful weight around. But it's not a hopelessness. I still get up, I still make my tea—Earl Grey, usually, with just a splash of milk—and I still feed the cat, Mr. Whiskers. I go through the motions of living. 

I just... I don't feel that profound sadness that makes you want to stay in bed and pull the covers over your head. It's more of an intense, vibrating sort of energy, if that makes sense. It's the constant worrying about what's going to happen next, and the guilt over what already happened. So, no, I wouldn't say I feel down or depressed most of the day. It's just this other, terrible mix of feelings that I can't seem to shake, no matter how hard I try or how many times people tell me I should be over it by now.

I mean, I... I have to, don't I? If I don't, I just fall apart. So, for instance, that intersection—the one downtown where it happened—I absolutely will not go near it. I will literally drive miles out of my way, taking all these convoluted back roads and side streets, just to make sure I don't even come within a few blocks of it. My sister thinks I'm being ridiculous, she says, \"It's just a street, you have to face it eventually,\" but she doesn't understand. She wasn't there. I used to love those walking tours, as I mentioned, but I've stopped signing up for them entirely. Any of them. Even the ones in completely different neighborhoods or focused on different things, like the historical architecture tours I used to enjoy so much. I just can't do it. And the community center... I used to go to their safety meetings, you know, the neighborhood watch and pedestrian safety groups? I was quite involved. But I had to stop going to those, too. I just cannot bear it when the conversations circle back to what happened there. Because they always do, don't they? People mean well, they want to discuss crosswalk safety or traffic calming measures, but to me, it's not an abstract policy discussion. It's my friend. It's that awful day. So I just stay home. I stay home, and I avoid the tours, and I avoid the meetings, and I avoid that whole side of town, because it's the only way I can manage to get through the day without completely breaking down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any ongoing negative thoughts about yourself, other people, or the world, especially since your friend’s death?\n\nPatient: Oh, constantly. It's... it's like a broken record in my head, doctor, just playing the same awful tune over and over until I think I might lose my mind. I just sit there in my armchair, sometimes for hours on end, and I keep going over and over the choices we made that afternoon. I keep telling myself that if I had just picked a different route—we could have walked down Elm Street instead, where the sidewalks are wider, or we could have just stayed inside the park boundaries where there are no cars at all. \n\nAnd that crossing... I knew it was a busy intersection. I knew it. I keep thinking that I should have pushed harder about it. I should have grabbed her arm, or said, \"Let's wait for the next light,\" or \"Let's walk down to the crosswalk with the better signal.\" If I had just done that, if I had just been a little more forceful or paid a bit more attention to where we were stepping, she might still be alive right now. We'd probably be having tea this afternoon like we always did on Tuesdays. \n\nBut then... well, then the guilt sort of twists, doesn't it? It twists into this absolutely blinding anger. I get furious, just so intensely angry all over again at that driver. How could he not be paying attention? What on earth was so important that he couldn't look out for two older women crossing the street in broad daylight? And the town, too! I mean, the local council, the city planners—I get furious at them for letting it happen. They know that intersection is a nightmare. We've talked about it at those very safety meetings I used to go to! They knew the sightlines were terrible, they knew the delivery trucks moved too fast around that corner, and they just... they did absolutely nothing to fix it. They let it happen. And I just cycle through it all—blaming myself for the route, hating the driver, resenting the town council... it just never stops spinning in my head. I look at the world now and I just see incompetence and carelessness everywhere, and I can't seem to find any peace with people or myself anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this constant guilt, fear, and anger—since your friend’s death?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't stopped, doctor. Not for a single day since it happened. It’s been... oh, let me think, it was late summer when we went on that tour, so it’s been about two years and three months now. Two years and three months since she was killed on that walking tour. And you'd think, wouldn't you, that after more than two years, the edge would wear off just a little bit? That maybe I’d wake up one morning and the first thought in my head wouldn't be the sound of that truck or the sight of her stepping off the curb. But it doesn't. It just doesn't fade. \n\nEvery single day for twenty-seven months, I’ve carried this heavy, dreadful feeling right here in my chest. And people say, \"Oh, time heals all wounds,\" but they have absolutely no idea what they are talking about. How can time heal it when the guilt is just as fresh as it was that very afternoon? I still sit in my chair, just like I did the week after the funeral, going over the exact same \"what ifs\" and \"if onlys.\" And the anger... well, the anger might actually be worse now than it was at the beginning. Because at the beginning, you're just in shock, aren't you? You're just trying to plan a funeral and figure out how to tell people. But now, two years and three months later, the shock is gone, and all I'm left with is this boiling rage at the driver, at the city council, at myself... and the fear, of course. The absolute terror of just walking down a normal street. It's been two years and three months of this, doctor, and I honestly don't know how much longer I can keep living like this. It's exhausting. It is just completely exhausting to carry this around every minute of every day since that afternoon.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way—this constant guilt, fear, and anger— every day since your friend’s death, without any significant breaks or relief?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, I suppose that's exactly what I've been trying to say. Every single day. I mean, it's not like I don't try to find some relief, doctor, I really do. I try to distract myself. It's not like I have a good week and then a bad week. It's just... a constant, heavy blanket over everything. I stay home, and I avoid the tours, and I avoid the meetings, and I avoid that whole side of town, because it's the only way I can manage to get through the day without completely breaking down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any ongoing negative thoughts about yourself, other people, or the world, especially since your friend’s death?\n\nPatient: Oh, constantly. It's... it's like a broken record in my head, doctor, just playing the same awful tune over and over until I think I might lose my mind. I just sit there in my armchair, sometimes for hours on end, and I keep going over and over the choices we made that afternoon. I keep telling myself that if I had just picked a different route—we could have walked down Elm Street instead, where the sidewalks are wider, or we could have just stayed inside the park boundaries where there are no cars at all. \n\nAnd that crossing... I knew it was a busy intersection. I knew it. I keep thinking that I should have pushed harder about it. I should have grabbed her arm, or said, \"Let's wait for the next light,\" or \"Let's walk down to the crosswalk with the better signal.\" If I had just done that, if I had just been a little more forceful or paid a bit more attention to where we were stepping, she might still be alive right now. We'd probably be having tea this afternoon like we always did on Tuesdays. \n\nBut then... well, then the guilt sort of twists, doesn't it? It twists into this absolutely blinding anger. I get furious, just so intensely angry all over again at that driver. How could he not be paying attention? What on earth was so important that he couldn't look out for two older women crossing the street in broad daylight? And the town, too! I mean, the local council, the city planners—I get furious at them for letting it happen. They know that intersection is a nightmare. We've talked about it at those very safety meetings I used to go to! They knew the sightlines were terrible, they knew the delivery trucks moved too fast around that corner, and they just... they did absolutely nothing to fix it. They let it happen. And I just cycle through it all—blaming myself for the route, hating the driver, resenting the town council... it just never stops spinning in my head. I look at the world now and I just see incompetence and carelessness everywhere, and I can't seem to find any peace with people or myself anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this constant guilt, fear, and anger—since your friend’s death?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't stopped, doctor. Not for a single day since it happened. It’s been... oh, let me think, it was late summer when we went on that tour, so it’s been about two years and three months now. Two years and three months since she was killed on that walking tour. And you'd think, wouldn't you, that after more than two years, the edge would wear off just a little bit? That maybe I’d wake up one morning and the first thought in my head wouldn't be the sound of that truck or the sight of her stepping off the curb. But it doesn't. It just doesn't fade. \n\nEvery single day for twenty-seven months, I’ve carried this heavy, dreadful feeling right here in my chest. And people say, \"Oh, time heals all wounds,\" but they have absolutely no idea what they are talking about. How can time heal it when the guilt is just as fresh as it was that very afternoon? I still sit in my chair, just like I did the week after the funeral, going over the exact same \"what ifs\" and \"if onlys.\" And the anger... well, the anger might actually be worse now than it was at the beginning. Because at the beginning, you're just in shock, aren't you? You're just trying to plan a funeral and figure out how to tell people. But now, two years and three months later, the shock is gone, and all I'm left with is this boiling rage at the driver, at the city council, at myself... and the fear, of course. The absolute terror of just walking down a normal street. It's been two years and three months of this, doctor, and I honestly don't know how much longer I can keep living like this. It's exhausting. It is just completely exhausting to carry this around every minute of every day since that afternoon.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way—this constant guilt, fear, and anger— every day since your friend’s death, without any significant breaks or relief?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, I suppose that's exactly what I've been trying to say. Every single day. I mean, it's not like I don't try to find some relief, doctor, I really do. I try to distract myself. I'll sit down with a book—I used to be such an avid reader, mysteries mostly, Agatha Christie, that sort of thing—but I'll read the same paragraph over and over and realize I haven't absorbed a single word because my mind is right back at that intersection. Or I'll turn on the television, just for some background noise, hoping maybe a cooking show or a documentary will take my mind off it, but the relief just never comes. \n\nThere's no break. It's not like I have a good week and then a bad week. It's just... a constant, heavy blanket over everything. Even when I'm sleeping, if you can call it sleeping, I wake up with my heart pounding and that same terrible feeling in the pit of my stomach. My sister—the one who thinks I'm being ridiculous about avoiding the street—she tells me I need to \"take a holiday\" or \"find a new hobby\" to give myself a break. As if a weekend at the coast is going to magically erase the fact that my dearest friend was killed right in front of me while we were just out for a walk! It's so frustrating trying to explain it to people who haven't lived it. They think you can just switch it off for a few hours and have a nice cup of tea and forget about it. But you can't. You just can't. It's always there, humming in the background, this awful mixture of feeling responsible and being terrified and just so incredibly angry. Every day. Every single day for two years and three months. There hasn't been a single day of peace since it happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy, or have you found that you can’t feel pleasure in things that used to make you happy?\n\nPatient: Yes... yes, that's exactly it. It's like all the color has just completely drained out of everything. I used to have such a full schedule, doctor, you wouldn't even believe it. My calendar was always marked up with different colored pens for all my different groups and projects. \n\nI used to spend hours in the late winter just planning my garden beds—mapping out exactly where the dahlias would go, making sure the hostas had enough shade, ordering seeds from those specialty catalogs... it gave me such a thrill to see it all come together. And then there were my quilting ladies. We'd get together every Thursday morning, bringing our scraps and our sewing machines, and we'd just piece together these beautiful tops for hours. We even organized these lovely theater outings once a month, getting a group rate for the matinees downtown. We saw some truly wonderful productions. \n\nAnd the book club! I mentioned earlier that I used to read a lot of mysteries, well, we had this little group and I used to just love it. I would argue happily—just so passionately—with the other women about the plot holes or who the killer was in whatever book we were reading that month. We'd sit around and just debate the clues, having such a marvelous time. \n\nBut now... now I just don't care. I look at those same things on the calendar—well, the old calendars, because I hardly bother writing things down anymore—and I feel almost nothing. It's just completely blank inside. The ladies still call, bless them, they ask if I'm coming back to the quilt group, or if I want a ticket to the new play, and I just make an excuse. Because I look at those activities, those things that used to fill my days with so much joy, and it's like... it's like they belong to someone else entirely. Like they belong to someone I used to be, before that afternoon. The thought of sitting in a theater or arguing over a fictional story just feels so utterly pointless now. I just can't muster a single ounce of pleasure for any of it.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe your mood most days—are you mostly sad, angry, or do your emotions shift between different states?\n\nPatient: Well, it is not really a shift, doctor, not like a pendulum swinging back and forth between different things. It is more like a constant, awful mixture of all of them at once. What really brings me in here to see you, what I have been trying to make everyone understand, is that I have been living with this constant mix of guilt, fear, and anger since my friend was killed. It is all of them, all the time, just sitting right here heavily in my chest. \n\nI wake up in the morning, and the fear is right there, the fear of stepping outside, of the traffic, of just existing in a world where something so terrible can happen in the blink of an eye. And then the guilt just washes over me, because I was there, wasn't I? I was right beside her, and I lived, and she didn't. I have spent hours, oh, countless hours, just sitting in my armchair in the living room, the one by the window with the floral upholstery, looking out at the street and feeling so incredibly guilty that I am the one still breathing. And the anger is always boiling underneath it all, anger at the driver, anger at the whole situation. \n\nAnd the thing is, doctor, it just has not eased. It has not eased at all, not the way everyone seemed to think it would. People have these timelines in their heads, you see. They think that after a year, I should be moving on, or that after two years, surely I am finding my peace by now. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she actually had the nerve to tell me a few months ago that I needed to let go of the past because holding onto it was only hurting me. Can you imagine? As if I am holding onto this on purpose! As if I want to wake up every single day feeling this dreadful combination of being terrified and furious and so very guilty. They just do not understand that it does not just wash away. It is a constant state of being. I am just stuck in it, every single day since that awful afternoon.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel down, depressed, or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not... not exactly depressed or hopeless, doctor. I mean, I don't sit around crying all day or feeling like life is completely over, like there's no future at all. It's not that deep, dark sadness that you hear people talk about when they say they're depressed. It's just... well, like I was saying, it's the fear and the guilt and the anger that are so exhausting. \n\nI don't wake up feeling down in the dumps, exactly. I wake up feeling on edge. My sister, she called me just the other day—Tuesday, I think it was, right before lunch—and she asked me if I was feeling depressed because I hadn't returned her call from the weekend. And I had to tell her, \"Margaret, I'm not depressed, I'm just tired.\" I'm so very tired of carrying this awful weight around. But it's not a hopelessness. I still get up, I still make my tea—Earl Grey, usually, with just a splash of milk—and I still feed the cat, Mr. Whiskers. I go through the motions of living. \n\nI just... I don't feel that profound sadness that makes you want to stay in bed and pull the covers over your head. It's more of an intense, vibrating sort of energy, if that makes sense. It's the constant worrying about what's going to happen next, and the guilt over what already happened. So, no, I wouldn't say I feel down or depressed most of the day. It's just this other, terrible mix of feelings that I can't seem to shake, no matter how hard I try or how many times people tell me I should be over it by now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

It's just this other, terrible mix of feelings that I can't seem to shake, no matter how hard I try or how many times people tell me I should be over it by now.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you found yourself getting easily irritated or having sudden outbursts of anger since your friend’s death?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 69 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Irritability_Aggression\nmanifestation: \"I snap at neighbors over small things like a car idling too long or someone blocking the sidewalk, and afterward I am embarrassed because the sharpness in my voice sounds nothing like me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, I suppose I have. And it's... it's so upsetting, doctor, because it's just not who I am. Or who I was. I've always prided myself on being neighborly, you know? Keeping a pleasant demeanor, greeting everyone with a smile when I go out to get the mail. But lately, I just... I have this incredibly short fuse. 

Just last week, for instance, there was a car idling out front. It was the delivery driver for the people across the street—the young couple who moved in last spring, they get so many packages, it's a wonder they have room for furniture in that house. Anyway, the driver left his engine running while he carried the boxes up to the porch, and the sound of it, that low rumble, it just set my teeth on edge. Before I even knew what I was doing, I was out the front door, marching down the steps in my slippers, and I just snapped at him. I raised my voice, telling him he was polluting the neighborhood and making a racket, and that he needed to turn the engine off right this instant. The poor man looked at me like I was completely unhinged. 

And then there was an incident just two days ago. I was walking down the sidewalk to drop a letter in the postbox at the corner, and a teenager—one of the Miller boys, I think—had left his bicycle right in the middle of the path. Now, normally, I would just step around it, or perhaps move it to the grass. But I saw it, and this flash of absolute fury just went through me. I actually yelled at him from across the yard. I told him he was being thoughtless and dangerous, blocking the walkway like that. 

And the terrible thing is, doctor, as soon as the words are out of my mouth, as soon as I hear that sharp, harsh tone in my voice, I feel so incredibly embarrassed. Because it sounds nothing like me. It sounds like some bitter, angry old woman, and that's not who I am. But in that split second, the irritation is just so overwhelming, so intense, I can't seem to stop myself from lashing out over these tiny, insignificant things. It's like I have no patience left, not even a drop of it.

I look at the world now and I just see incompetence and carelessness everywhere, and I can't seem to find any peace with people or myself anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this constant guilt, fear, and anger—since your friend’s death?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't stopped, doctor. Not for a single day since it happened. It’s been... oh, let me think, it was late summer when we went on that tour, so it’s been about two years and three months now. Two years and three months since she was killed on that walking tour. And you'd think, wouldn't you, that after more than two years, the edge would wear off just a little bit? That maybe I’d wake up one morning and the first thought in my head wouldn't be the sound of that truck or the sight of her stepping off the curb. But it doesn't. It just doesn't fade. \n\nEvery single day for twenty-seven months, I’ve carried this heavy, dreadful feeling right here in my chest. The absolute terror of just walking down a normal street. It's been two years and three months of this, doctor, and I honestly don't know how much longer I can keep living like this. It's exhausting. I just can't muster a single ounce of pleasure for any of it.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe your mood most days—are you mostly sad, angry, or do your emotions shift between different states?\n\nPatient: Well, it is not really a shift, doctor, not like a pendulum swinging back and forth between different things. I stay home, and I avoid the tours, and I avoid the meetings, and I avoid that whole side of town, because it's the only way I can manage to get through the day without completely breaking down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any ongoing negative thoughts about yourself, other people, or the world, especially since your friend’s death?\n\nPatient: Oh, constantly. It's... it's like a broken record in my head, doctor, just playing the same awful tune over and over until I think I might lose my mind. I just sit there in my armchair, sometimes for hours on end, and I keep going over and over the choices we made that afternoon. I keep telling myself that if I had just picked a different route—we could have walked down Elm Street instead, where the sidewalks are wider, or we could have just stayed inside the park boundaries where there are no cars at all. \n\nAnd that crossing... I knew it was a busy intersection. I knew it. I keep thinking that I should have pushed harder about it. I should have grabbed her arm, or said, \"Let's wait for the next light,\" or \"Let's walk down to the crosswalk with the better signal.\" If I had just done that, if I had just been a little more forceful or paid a bit more attention to where we were stepping, she might still be alive right now. We'd probably be having tea this afternoon like we always did on Tuesdays. \n\nBut then... well, then the guilt sort of twists, doesn't it? It twists into this absolutely blinding anger. I get furious, just so intensely angry all over again at that driver. How could he not be paying attention? What on earth was so important that he couldn't look out for two older women crossing the street in broad daylight? And the town, too! I mean, the local council, the city planners—I get furious at them for letting it happen. They know that intersection is a nightmare. We've talked about it at those very safety meetings I used to go to! They knew the sightlines were terrible, they knew the delivery trucks moved too fast around that corner, and they just... they did absolutely nothing to fix it. They let it happen. And I just cycle through it all—blaming myself for the route, hating the driver, resenting the town council... it just never stops spinning in my head. I look at the world now and I just see incompetence and carelessness everywhere, and I can't seem to find any peace with people or myself anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this constant guilt, fear, and anger—since your friend’s death?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't stopped, doctor. Not for a single day since it happened. It’s been... oh, let me think, it was late summer when we went on that tour, so it’s been about two years and three months now. Two years and three months since she was killed on that walking tour. And you'd think, wouldn't you, that after more than two years, the edge would wear off just a little bit? That maybe I’d wake up one morning and the first thought in my head wouldn't be the sound of that truck or the sight of her stepping off the curb. But it doesn't. It just doesn't fade. \n\nEvery single day for twenty-seven months, I’ve carried this heavy, dreadful feeling right here in my chest. And people say, \"Oh, time heals all wounds,\" but they have absolutely no idea what they are talking about. How can time heal it when the guilt is just as fresh as it was that very afternoon? I still sit in my chair, just like I did the week after the funeral, going over the exact same \"what ifs\" and \"if onlys.\" And the anger... well, the anger might actually be worse now than it was at the beginning. Because at the beginning, you're just in shock, aren't you? You're just trying to plan a funeral and figure out how to tell people. But now, two years and three months later, the shock is gone, and all I'm left with is this boiling rage at the driver, at the city council, at myself... and the fear, of course. The absolute terror of just walking down a normal street. It's been two years and three months of this, doctor, and I honestly don't know how much longer I can keep living like this. It's exhausting. It is just completely exhausting to carry this around every minute of every day since that afternoon.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way—this constant guilt, fear, and anger— every day since your friend’s death, without any significant breaks or relief?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, I suppose that's exactly what I've been trying to say. Every single day. I mean, it's not like I don't try to find some relief, doctor, I really do. I try to distract myself. I'll sit down with a book—I used to be such an avid reader, mysteries mostly, Agatha Christie, that sort of thing—but I'll read the same paragraph over and over and realize I haven't absorbed a single word because my mind is right back at that intersection. Or I'll turn on the television, just for some background noise, hoping maybe a cooking show or a documentary will take my mind off it, but the relief just never comes. \n\nThere's no break. It's not like I have a good week and then a bad week. It's just... a constant, heavy blanket over everything. Even when I'm sleeping, if you can call it sleeping, I wake up with my heart pounding and that same terrible feeling in the pit of my stomach. My sister—the one who thinks I'm being ridiculous about avoiding the street—she tells me I need to \"take a holiday\" or \"find a new hobby\" to give myself a break. As if a weekend at the coast is going to magically erase the fact that my dearest friend was killed right in front of me while we were just out for a walk! It's so frustrating trying to explain it to people who haven't lived it. They think you can just switch it off for a few hours and have a nice cup of tea and forget about it. But you can't. You just can't. It's always there, humming in the background, this awful mixture of feeling responsible and being terrified and just so incredibly angry. Every day. Every single day for two years and three months. There hasn't been a single day of peace since it happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy, or have you found that you can’t feel pleasure in things that used to make you happy?\n\nPatient: Yes... yes, that's exactly it. It's like all the color has just completely drained out of everything. I used to have such a full schedule, doctor, you wouldn't even believe it. My calendar was always marked up with different colored pens for all my different groups and projects. \n\nI used to spend hours in the late winter just planning my garden beds—mapping out exactly where the dahlias would go, making sure the hostas had enough shade, ordering seeds from those specialty catalogs... it gave me such a thrill to see it all come together. And then there were my quilting ladies. We'd get together every Thursday morning, bringing our scraps and our sewing machines, and we'd just piece together these beautiful tops for hours. We even organized these lovely theater outings once a month, getting a group rate for the matinees downtown. We saw some truly wonderful productions. \n\nAnd the book club! I mentioned earlier that I used to read a lot of mysteries, well, we had this little group and I used to just love it. I would argue happily—just so passionately—with the other women about the plot holes or who the killer was in whatever book we were reading that month. We'd sit around and just debate the clues, having such a marvelous time. \n\nBut now... now I just don't care. I look at those same things on the calendar—well, the old calendars, because I hardly bother writing things down anymore—and I feel almost nothing. It's just completely blank inside. The ladies still call, bless them, they ask if I'm coming back to the quilt group, or if I want a ticket to the new play, and I just make an excuse. Because I look at those activities, those things that used to fill my days with so much joy, and it's like... it's like they belong to someone else entirely. Like they belong to someone I used to be, before that afternoon. The thought of sitting in a theater or arguing over a fictional story just feels so utterly pointless now. I just can't muster a single ounce of pleasure for any of it.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe your mood most days—are you mostly sad, angry, or do your emotions shift between different states?\n\nPatient: Well, it is not really a shift, doctor, not like a pendulum swinging back and forth between different things. It is more like a constant, awful mixture of all of them at once. What really brings me in here to see you, what I have been trying to make everyone understand, is that I have been living with this constant mix of guilt, fear, and anger since my friend was killed. It is all of them, all the time, just sitting right here heavily in my chest. \n\nI wake up in the morning, and the fear is right there, the fear of stepping outside, of the traffic, of just existing in a world where something so terrible can happen in the blink of an eye. And then the guilt just washes over me, because I was there, wasn't I? I was right beside her, and I lived, and she didn't. I have spent hours, oh, countless hours, just sitting in my armchair in the living room, the one by the window with the floral upholstery, looking out at the street and feeling so incredibly guilty that I am the one still breathing. And the anger is always boiling underneath it all, anger at the driver, anger at the whole situation. \n\nAnd the thing is, doctor, it just has not eased. It has not eased at all, not the way everyone seemed to think it would. People have these timelines in their heads, you see. They think that after a year, I should be moving on, or that after two years, surely I am finding my peace by now. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she actually had the nerve to tell me a few months ago that I needed to let go of the past because holding onto it was only hurting me. Can you imagine? As if I am holding onto this on purpose! As if I want to wake up every single day feeling this dreadful combination of being terrified and furious and so very guilty. They just do not understand that it does not just wash away. It is a constant state of being. I am just stuck in it, every single day since that awful afternoon.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel down, depressed, or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not... not exactly depressed or hopeless, doctor. I mean, I don't sit around crying all day or feeling like life is completely over, like there's no future at all. It's not that deep, dark sadness that you hear people talk about when they say they're depressed. It's just... well, like I was saying, it's the fear and the guilt and the anger that are so exhausting. \n\nI don't wake up feeling down in the dumps, exactly. I wake up feeling on edge. My sister, she called me just the other day—Tuesday, I think it was, right before lunch—and she asked me if I was feeling depressed because I hadn't returned her call from the weekend. And I had to tell her, \"Margaret, I'm not depressed, I'm just tired.\" I'm so very tired of carrying this awful weight around. But it's not a hopelessness. I still get up, I still make my tea—Earl Grey, usually, with just a splash of milk—and I still feed the cat, Mr. Whiskers. I go through the motions of living. \n\nI just... I don't feel that profound sadness that makes you want to stay in bed and pull the covers over your head. It's more of an intense, vibrating sort of energy, if that makes sense. It's the constant worrying about what's going to happen next, and the guilt over what already happened. So, no, I wouldn't say I feel down or depressed most of the day. It's just this other, terrible mix of feelings that I can't seem to shake, no matter how hard I try or how many times people tell me I should be over it by now.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself getting easily irritated or having sudden outbursts of anger since your friend’s death?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. And it's... it's so upsetting, doctor, because it's just not who I am. Or who I was. I've always prided myself on being neighborly, you know? Keeping a pleasant demeanor, greeting everyone with a smile when I go out to get the mail. But lately, I just... I have this incredibly short fuse. \n\nJust last week, for instance, there was a car idling out front. It was the delivery driver for the people across the street—the young couple who moved in last spring, they get so many packages, it's a wonder they have room for furniture in that house. Anyway, the driver left his engine running while he carried the boxes up to the porch, and the sound of it, that low rumble, it just set my teeth on edge. Before I even knew what I was doing, I was out the front door, marching down the steps in my slippers, and I just snapped at him. I raised my voice, telling him he was polluting the neighborhood and making a racket, and that he needed to turn the engine off right this instant. The poor man looked at me like I was completely unhinged. \n\nAnd then there was an incident just two days ago. I was walking down the sidewalk to drop a letter in the postbox at the corner, and a teenager—one of the Miller boys, I think—had left his bicycle right in the middle of the path. Now, normally, I would just step around it, or perhaps move it to the grass. But I saw it, and this flash of absolute fury just went through me. I actually yelled at him from across the yard. I told him he was being thoughtless and dangerous, blocking the walkway like that. \n\nAnd the terrible thing is, doctor, as soon as the words are out of my mouth, as soon as I hear that sharp, harsh tone in my voice, I feel so incredibly embarrassed. Because it sounds nothing like me. It sounds like some bitter, angry old woman, and that's not who I am. But in that split second, the irritation is just so overwhelming, so intense, I can't seem to stop myself from lashing out over these tiny, insignificant things. It's like I have no patience left, not even a drop of it."
